the beginning of the document in [[f:docMetaParser]], because although the link may
refer to a block that has a parent heading which has its own OID, we don't have
that information at hand.

#+name: f:oidReferenceParser
#+begin_src haskell
oidReferenceParser :: [Char] -> [Char] -> OrgParser (OID, Text)
oidReferenceParser disallowedChars ending =
  MP.choice
    [ MP.try explicitFQCN,
      MP.try explicitOID,
      MP.try implicit
    ]
  where
    explicitOID :: OrgParser (OID, Text)
    explicitOID = do
      _ <- string "id:"
      oidText <-
        MP.some $ do
          _ <- MP.notFollowedBy . string $ T.pack ending
          MP.noneOf $ ':' : disallowedChars
      oid <- validateOidText $ T.pack oidText
      pure (oid, "")
    explicitFQCN = do
      (oid, _) <- explicitOID
      _ <- string "::"
      name <- targetName
      pure (oid, name)
    implicit = do
      orgParserState <- get
      name <- targetName
      pure (orgParserState.docOid, name)
    targetName :: OrgParser Text
    targetName = compressedTextParser disallowedChars ending
#+end_src

The [[f:compressedTextParser]] is more lenient about how whitespace is interpreted,
so that if the user wants to refer to a target that has spaces in it, they can
write that target over multiple lines. That is, a user may refer to a target
name like ~foo bar baz~ in the following ways:

#+begin_src org
[[foo bar
baz]]

[[foo
bar
baz]]

[[foo
bar baz]]
#+end_src

All of the above implicit OID links refer to the same ~foo bar baz~ target, even
though they are written slightly differently.

#+name: f:compressedTextParser
#+begin_src haskell
compressedTextParser :: [Char] -> [Char] -> OrgParser Text
compressedTextParser disallowedChars ending = do
  orgParserState <- get
  tn <-
    proseParser'
      $ proseSpaceConsumer'
        orgParserState.insideCodeBlock
  pure $ compressWhitespace tn
  where
    compressWhitespace = T.unwords . T.words
    proseSpaceConsumer' :: Bool -> OrgParser Text -- (ref:proseSpaceConsumer')
    proseSpaceConsumer' insideCodeBlock
      | insideCodeBlock = spaces
      | otherwise =
          MP.choice
            [ string "\n",
              spaces
            ]
      where
        spaces :: OrgParser Text
        spaces = MP.takeWhile1P (Just "space") (\c -> c == ' ')
    proseParser' :: OrgParser Text -> OrgParser Text
    proseParser' spaceConsumer = do
      a <- MP.choice baseParsers
      b <- MP.many $ MP.choice (spaceParser : baseParsers)
      pure . T.concat $ a : b
      where
        spaceParser = whitespaceCompressor spaceConsumer
        baseParsers =
          [ textParser'
          ]
    textParser' :: OrgParser Text
    textParser' = do
      ws <-
        MP.some
          $ MP.choice
            [ MP.label "normal characters" normalChars
            ]
      pure $ T.concat ws
      where
        normalChars :: OrgParser Text
        normalChars = do
          cs <- MP.some $ normalChar
          pure $ T.pack cs
        normalChar :: OrgParser Char
        normalChar = do
          _ <- MP.notFollowedBy . string $ T.pack ending
          MP.satisfy isNormalChar
        isNormalChar c =
          notElem c disallowedChars
            && (not $ isSpace c)
#+end_src

Unlike [[f:proseSpaceConsumer]], the ~proseSpaceConsumer'~ [[(proseSpaceConsumer')]] does
*not* check for the correctness of the indentation following a newline (it does
not take an ~expectedIndentLen~ parameter). This is because we want to be more
generous for the case where OID links are found in prose, because sometimes
indentation can get silently added when using =fill-paragraph= in Emacs (if the
OID link target gets unlucky and ends up getting hard wrapped by
=fill-paragraph=). Otherwise, this parser will fail, which would then make the OID
link become wrongly parsed.

Now we could punish the user for having erroneous indentation, but because
=org-toggle-link-display= can hide this error caused by =fill-paragraph= (and
because the link target contents will be hidden), we take the approach of being
a bit lenient here. We still disallow newlines inside the link target inside
code blocks, which is why ~insideCodeBlock~ is still a parameter for
~proseSpaceConsumer'~. See [[ti:0006-links-oids-lenient-whitespace]].

Below we check a typical case with some Noweb references in it. Note that we
have to set =#+header: :noweb off= here because otherwise Orgmode will think
=<<foo>>= and =<<bar>>= are Noweb references that exist in this doc (=lilac.org=), and
because they don't exist, will just result in those strings not being inserted
into [[file:../test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-blocks-noweb]].

#+name: ti:0007-blocks-noweb
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-blocks-noweb
#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src org
,#+begin_src sh
<<foo>>

<<quux
>>
echo <<bar>> x y z
,#+end_src
#+end_src

The ~<<quux~ is not interpreted as a Noweb reference, because it doesn't end
with ~>>~ on the same line.

#+name: t:0007-blocks-noweb
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0007-blocks-noweb"
  ( nilOrgDoc
      <<Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-noweb>>
      []
  )
  defaultOrgParserState
    { anonBlockNumber = 1
    }
#+end_src

#+name: Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-noweb
#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src haskell
[ CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__anon_block_number", "1"),
              ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh")
            ],
        body =
          [ BTokenLinkTarget
              $ LinkTargetOid
                LinkOid
                  { oid = nilOid,
                    name = "foo"
                  },
            BTokenString "\n\n<<quux\n>>\necho ",
            BTokenLinkTarget
              $ LinkTargetOid
                LinkOid
                  { oid = nilOid,
                    name = "bar"
                  },
            BTokenString " x y z"
          ]
      }
]
#+end_src

We have to set =#+header: :noweb off= in [[Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-noweb]] as
well, to avoid the ~<<quux\n>>~ bit from becoming interpreted as a Noweb
reference.

The test below checks the ~nowebOff~ boolean behavior from [[f:blockParser]]. That is,
if Lilac sees =#+header: :noweb off=, it should not treat text that look like
=<<foo>>= as a Noweb link, but as a literal =<<foo>>= string.

#+name: ti:0007-blocks-noweb-off
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-blocks-noweb-off
#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src org
,#+header: :noweb off
,#+begin_src sh
<<foo>>

<<quux
>>
echo <<bar>> x y z
,#+end_src
#+end_src

#+name: t:0007-blocks-noweb-off
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0007-blocks-noweb-off"
  ( nilOrgDoc
      <<Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-noweb-off>>
      []
  )
  defaultOrgParserState
    { anonBlockNumber = 1
    }
#+end_src

#+name: Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-noweb-off
#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src haskell
[ CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__anon_block_number", "1"),
              ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("noweb", "off")
            ],
        body =
          [ BTokenString "<<foo>>\n\n<<quux\n>>\necho <<bar>> x y z"
          ]
      }
]
#+end_src

Because Lilac relies heavily on Noweb references (links), it will be turned on
by default. So the only way to disable them would be to set =#+header: :noweb off=
for each block where you'd want it disabled.

Sometimes we'll have Noweb references surrounded by punctuation. For example,
see how we use ~<<GHC version>>~ in [[id:65e5513b-2167-44d6-bd3a-50fdd9ee020e::nix:ghcVersion][nix:ghcVersion]]. This is a typical use case
when the referenced block only has 1 line of text in it and we want to inject
those contents into an existing line.

#+name: ti:0007-blocks-noweb-intraline
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-blocks-noweb-intraline
#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src org
,#+begin_src sh
(<<foo>>) and "<<bar>>" and <<<ok>>> <<a->b>>
,#+end_src
#+end_src

#+name: t:0007-blocks-noweb-intraline
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0007-blocks-noweb-intraline"
  ( nilOrgDoc
      <<Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-noweb-intraline>>
      []
  )
  defaultOrgParserState
    { anonBlockNumber = 1
    }
#+end_src

#+name: Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-noweb-intraline
#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src haskell
[ CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__anon_block_number", "1"),
              ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh")
            ],
        body =
          [ BTokenString "(",
            BTokenLinkTarget
              $ LinkTargetOid
                LinkOid
                  { oid = nilOid,
                    name = "foo"
                  },
            BTokenString ") and \"",
            BTokenLinkTarget
              $ LinkTargetOid
                LinkOid
                  { oid = nilOid,
                    name = "bar"
                  },
            BTokenString "\" and <",
            BTokenLinkTarget
              $ LinkTargetOid
                LinkOid
                  { oid = nilOid,
                    name = "ok"
                  },
            BTokenString "> ",
            BTokenLinkTarget
              $ LinkTargetOid
                LinkOid
                  { oid = nilOid,
                    name = "a->b"
                  }
          ]
      }
]
#+end_src

** Add padding between consecutive Noweb links
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       51f2edd8-5f8e-4c63-841f-da6c5c41680a
:END:

When we eventually tangle blocks, we want to inject some padding (a newline)
between Noweb links because each Noweb block is its own "unit" and it makes
sense to add some separation between consecutive Noweb blocks, to get some
visual separation in the final output.

We only want to add this padding for cases where there are /consecutive/ Noweb
links. For example, in

#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src txt
<<a>>

<<b>>
<<c>>
<<d>>

  <<e>>
  <<f>>

<<g>>
#+end_src

only ~<<b>>~ ~<<c>>~ ~<<d>>~ and ~<<e>>~ ~<<f>>~ are consecutive. And in the final tangled
output we would want to make it as if we had originally written

#+header: :noweb off
#+begin_src txt
<<a>>

<<b>>

<<c>>

<<d>>

  <<e>>

  <<f>>

<<g>>
#+end_src

This allows us to write in a bit more compact manner in the Org source document,
while still giving each Noweb link (and associated block) some separation from
the other blocks in the tangled output.

The way we do this automatic padding is by injecting newlines into the parsed
block's body ~BToken~ values, as ~BTokenString "\n"~. This is what we do by calling
[[f:padConsecutiveNowebLinks]] in [[f:blockParser]].

The high-level algorithm is as follows:

  1) Concatenate the ~BToken~ values together (using the ~Text~ representation). For
     ~BTokenString~ values we use the inner text information as is. For
     ~BTokenLinkTarget~ we use the target, but also inject a special [[link-marker]]
     which uses an ASCII ~NUL~ character.

  2) Split the contents by newline.

  3) Group together the lines that have Noweb links. In other words, consecutive
     lines that have Noweb links are grouped together.

  4) Within each group, only mark the links at the end of the line (because
     there may be more than one link in a line) as needing a newline. By marking
     links as needing (or not needing) a newline, we can easily inject newlines
     at the right spots in the next step.

     We can't add a newline for the very last line in the group though, because
     that would be superfluous (we want to add newlines *between* consecutive
     blocks, not after every consecutive block).

  5) Using the information from the previous step, iterate through all ~BToken~
     values and whenever we see a ~BTokenLinkTarget~, inject the newline (if the
     link was marked as needing a newline).

The markers are just simple boolean values (one for each Noweb link found in the
block).

Now let's look at the implementation in detail. Step 1 is done in
[[f:getBlockContents]].

#+name: f:getBlockContents
#+begin_src haskell
getBlockContents :: [BToken] -> Text
getBlockContents blockBody = T.concat $ map bTokenToText blockBody
  where
    bTokenToText :: BToken -> Text
    bTokenToText = \case
      BTokenString text -> text
      BTokenPadding -> ""
      BTokenLinkTarget (LinkTargetOid linkOid) ->
        <<link-marker>> <> (T.show linkOid)
      BTokenLinkTarget {} -> ""
#+end_src

The ~<<link-marker>>~ is just a special marker we use to keep track of where the
links are inside the concatenated (single) ~Text~ value representing all of the
block contents.

#+name: link-marker
#+begin_src haskell
"\NUL_link"
#+end_src

Steps 2, 3, and 4 are all done inside [[f:getLinkPaddingBools]]. This function
returns a list of booleans that correspond to each of the Noweb links found. If
~True~, this means the link needs a trailing newline (padding) added to it.

The line ~map (reverse . (zip $ False : repeat True) . reverse)~ says to only pad
the lines except for the last one, and ~distributeBools~ only pads the very last
link found in the line.

#+name: f:getLinkPaddingBools
#+begin_src haskell
getLinkPaddingBools :: [BToken] -> [Bool]
getLinkPaddingBools blockBody =
  concatMap (concatMap distributeBools)
    . map (reverse . (zip $ False : repeat True) . reverse)
    . groupBy consecutiveNowebLines
    . T.lines
    $ getBlockContents blockBody
  where
    consecutiveNowebLines :: Text -> Text -> Bool
    consecutiveNowebLines a b = (countLinks a > 0) && (countLinks b > 0)
    distributeBools (False, line) =
      replicate (countLinks line) False
    distributeBools (True, line) =
      case countLinks line of
        1 -> [True]
        n -> reverse $ True : replicate (n - 1) False
    countLinks = T.count <<link-marker>>
#+end_src

Now that we have a list of booleans to mark whether a link needs a newline
appended to it, all we have to do is go through all of the links in the block,
adding a ~BTokenPaddding~ every time we see a ~True~ boolean (~padLink~).

#+name: f:padConsecutiveNowebLinks
#+begin_src haskell
padConsecutiveNowebLinks :: [BToken] -> [BToken]
padConsecutiveNowebLinks blockBody =
  reverse
    . fst
    $ foldl' f ([], getLinkPaddingBools blockBody) blockBody
  where
    f (acc, bools) text@(BTokenString _) = (text : acc, bools)
    f (acc, bools) BTokenPadding = (acc, bools)
    f (acc, bools) linkTarget@(BTokenLinkTarget _) =
      case bools of
        (padLink : rest)
          | padLink -> (BTokenPadding : linkTarget : acc, rest)
          | otherwise -> (linkTarget : acc, rest)
        [] -> (linkTarget : acc, [])
#+end_src

See [[id:c78d3f21-b295-4f19-b720-ab50dedb3252::t:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links][t:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links]] for an example of this padding
behavior.

** Additional tests

A =#+begin_src= without an accompanying language should trigger an error.

#+name: ti:0007-block-src-without-lang
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-block-src-without-lang
#+begin_src org
,#+begin_src
,#+end_src
#+end_src

#+name: t:0007-block-src-without-lang
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0007-block-src-without-lang"
  "block type src requires a language specifier"
  11
#+end_src

Below we test the edge cases around detecting the end of a block.

#+name: ti:0007-blocks-ending
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-blocks-ending
#+begin_src org
,#+name: empty
,#+begin_src sh
,#+end_src

,#+name: end not in beginning of line
,#+begin_src sh
 ,#+end_src
,#+end_src

,#+name: end has no preceding blank line
,#+begin_src sh
foo
,#+end_src

,#+name: end has preceding blank line
,#+begin_src sh
foo

,#+end_src

,#+name: end has preceding newline (multiple)
,#+begin_src sh
foo


,#+end_src

,#+name: ignore #+end_example because this is a src block
,#+begin_src sh
,#+end_example
,#+end_src
#+end_src

#+name: t:0007-blocks-ending
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0007-blocks-ending"
  ( nilOrgDoc
      <<Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-ending>>
      []
  )
  defaultOrgParserState
    { cellNames =
        Set.fromList
          [ "empty",
            "end has no preceding blank line",
            "end has preceding blank line",
            "end has preceding newline (multiple)",
            "end not in beginning of line",
            "ignore #+end_example because this is a src block"
          ]
    }
#+end_src

#+name: Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-ending
#+begin_src haskell
[ CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "empty")
            ],
        body = []
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end not in beginning of line")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString " #+end_src"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end has no preceding blank line")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "foo"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end has preceding blank line")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "foo\n"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end has preceding newline (multiple)")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "foo\n\n"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "ignore #+end_example because this is a src block")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "#+end_example"]
      }
]
#+end_src

* List items
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       58f69993-599d-4389-ba47-3056b1bb2350
:END:

A /list item/ is made up of a /list head/ and /list body/. Consider the following
list:

  1) a

     b

     - c

     d

  2) e

Here the =1)= and =2)= are list heads for an /ordered/ list. The single dash =-= before
"c" is also a list head, but for an /unordered/ list. The list body for =1)=
includes the paragraphs =a=, =b=, the nested list item =c=, and the paragraph =d=. There
are two list items for the top-level ordered list =1)= and =2)=.

List heads encode the structure of cells that sit in the list bodies. So list
items encode both structure and content. In this manner they are similar to
[[id:16a68a74-d4cc-41a4-ac77-cbca77de2a31][headings]]. However unlike headings, a list item can have multiple other cells
(paragraphs, tables, or even code blocks) in its body, whereas a heading can
only have a single line of prose.

Another difference is that a list item can have content for it /after/ backing out
of a nested list item. For example in the list shown above the ~d~ paragraph is
part of the ordered list item ~1)~ even though the nested item ~c~ is just above it;
this sort of structure is now allowed for headings. For example, in the
following situation

#+begin_src org
,* a
,** b
#+end_src

anything that comes after ~** b~ will be part of the ~** b~ heading; there is no way
to make it belong to ~* a~ (any such content must be placed /before/ ~** b~).

Below we have a look at some more detailed rules around how lists may be written
in an Org file.

** Detailed rules
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c
:END:

Below are some detailed rules around /ordered/ list items, themselves expressed as
ordered list items, which are important for parsing:

  1) List items start out with 2-space indentation. This makes them easier to
     read from the raw Org file. The amount of additional indentation (in order
     to have nested list items) depends on where the text began in the parent
     item. See items "5)" and "10)" below for examples.

     - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-no-indent]]

     - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-proper-indent]]

  2) List items must be separate from each other with a blank line. This makes
     them easier to read when reading the Org file in plain text.

     - [[ti:0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator]]

  3) Additional hard-wrapped lines for a list item must be aligned with the text
     of the first line. This list item is an example of such a line.

     - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body]]

     - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over]]

     - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body]]

     - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid]]

     - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-indent-levels]]

  4) List items can have more than one paragraph associated with them.

     This is an example of another paragraph which is still part of the "4)"
     list item, and has 5 spaces of indentation.

     - [[ti:0009-list-content-simple]]

  5) List items can be nested.

     1) This is a nested item, underneath "5)". It has 5 spaces of initial
        indentation, where the "1" in "1)" lines up with the column where the
        "L" in "5) List" in the parent item. Subsequent lines of this paragraph
        have 8 spaces of indentation to line up with the text above it.

     See:

     - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested]]

     - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text]]

     - [[ti:0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels]]

  6) Unlike Markdown, the numbers of the list item must increase by one if there
     are additional items at the same level. The first number of a level may
     start from any non-negative number; for example, 0, 1, 5, 12 are all valid
     starting numbers.

     - [[ti:0009-ordered-lists-require-increment]]

     - [[ti:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-0-or-1]]

  7) The format for ordered list items requires a closing parenthesis
     immediately after the number. So =1)= is valid, but =1.= is invalid.

     - [[ti:0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis]]

  8) Only numbers may be used to denote item position. You cannot use letters or
     Roman numerals.

     - [[ti:0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters]]

  9) There is no limit to the number of consecutive list items at the same
     level (technically, we use an =Int= type to encode the number, so it will
     eventually max out, but you'd need millions of items to hit this limit).

  10) The indentation of text changes slightly depending on the number of the
      item. For example, this line has 6 spaces of indentation, unlike the
      paragraphs in item "4)" above, which uses 5 spaces.

      1) Similarly, this child item has text which has 9 spaces of indentation,
         unlike the 8 spaces of indentation in the ordered child item under item
         "5)" above.

      See:

      - [[ti:0009-lists-require-precise-indentation]]

  11) There can only be up to 5 additional levels of nesting (for a total of 6
      levels of items). This is because excessive nesting is an anti-pattern.
      See the ~lilac_maxListLevel~ [[id:84edd33a-a146-4a46-ac0a-cd1c3d0926d3][Lilac extension]] for customizing this behavior.

      - [[ti:0009-list-nesting-max]]

      - [[ti:0009-list-nesting-max-8]]

      - [[ti:0009-lists-prohibited]]

  12) Ordered list items may have unordered list items (discussed next) as
      children, and vice versa. However you may not mix the two types of items
      at the same level.

      - [[ti:0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists]]

      - [[ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own]]

      - [[ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered]]

  13) A list item may include a table. However the table cannot be on the same
      line as the list item head. Below is an example of such a table:

      | foo | bar |
      |-----+-----|
      | x   | y   |

      - [[ti:0012-tables-inside-lists]]

  14) Similarly, a list item may include mathematical equations. However, such
      an equation must be on a separate line from the list item head, just like
      for tables (see Rule 13 above).

      \[
         a + b = c
      \]

      The equation above (and this paragraph) are still part of Rule 14.

      - [[ti:0010-standalone-math-inside-lists]]

  15) Lists may be empty. For example, the following child list is valid even
      though it does not have any content:

      1)

      2)

      3)

      See:

      - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested]]

Unordered list items use a single dash character =-=, and always use 2-space
indentation (if you are only using unordered list items for nesting). Below is
an example:

  - one

  - two

    - foo

    - bar

  - three

Here's an example of mixing ordered and unordered lists:

  - foo

  - bar

    1) one

    2) two

       - baz

       - quux

       Note how this paragraph is under item "2", because it is indented the
       same way as "two", not "quux", above.

    3) three

  - dog

You may mix ordered and unordered lists at the same indentation level, as long
as they are not consecutive (you need some intervening cells, such as the "quux"
paragraph below):

  - foo

    1) one

    2) two

    quux

    - a

    - b

** Indentation and =ListMarker=

Parsing list items is tricky because of meaningful indentation (whitespace).
Keeping track of the current level of indentation requires additional state
during parsing, which we encode with =Level= (used by =listLevels= in
[[data:OrgParserState]]).

#+name: data:Level
#+begin_src haskell
type Level :: Type
data Level = Level
  { indent :: Int,
    number :: Maybe Int
  }
  deriving stock (Eq, Show)
#+end_src

This keeps track of the indentations and the last seen number of each (nested)
level of indentation thus far during parsing. Whenever we see a list head like
=1)= or ~-~, we record the number used as well as the indentations we expect for the
list head and list body. The main point of =listLevels= is to ensure that the
input is correctly formatted, as well as to detect whenever we start nesting an
additional level, or if we back out (finish) one or more levels of nesting (when
we see dedented text).

As we observe how these =listLevels= change during parsing, we create "start" and
"end" markers for list items at a particular level, which are the values of the
[[data:ListMarker]] type. The =ListMarker= objects are parsed as their own cell as a
=CListItem= (see [[data:Cell]]).

#+name: data:ListMarker
#+begin_src haskell
type ListMarker :: Type
data ListMarker
  = LOrderedStart
  | LOrderedEnd
  | LUnorderedStart
  | LUnorderedEnd
  | LItemStart
  | LItemEnd
  deriving stock (Eq, Ord, Generic, Show)
  deriving anyclass (FromJSON, ToJSON)
#+end_src

We take this model from HTML, which uses =<ol>= and =</ol>= to encode the start and
end of ordered lists (=<ul>= and =</ul>= for unordered lists), and =<li>= and =</li>= to
mark the start and en of a single list item.

#+name: tbl:List markers
#+caption: ~ListMarker~ values and their HTML equivalents.
| ListMarker      | HTML equivalent |
|-----------------+-----------------|
| ~LOrderedStart~   | ~<ol>~            |
| ~LOrderedEnd~     | ~</ol>~           |
| ~LUnorderedStart~ | ~<ul>~            |
| ~LUnorderedEnd~   | ~</ul>~           |
| ~LItemStart~      | ~<li>~            |
| ~LItemEnd~        | ~</li>~           |

In summary when we parse a list item, we parse it as a combination of =CListItem=
and other cell types for the list structure and content, respectively. This lets
us parse things into a single global list of cells in [[f:cellParser]], without a
need to nest them inside a recursive data structure (as shown in
https://markkarpov.com/tutorial/megaparsec.html#nested-indented-list).

** Parsing initial indentation to close list items
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5990dd7f-2f37-4a9e-96ea-6cc6b2e20dc0
:END:

Before getting into parsing the list items and taking into account all of the
[[id:32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c][detailed rules]], let's first consider case where we just want to /close off/ the
list items, as this is simpler than creating list items. We want to do this
every time we parse initial indentation where there is no list head. For
example, in the following

  1) a

     - b

     c

the indentation for paragraph =c= should by itself close off the preceding
unordered list item (~- b~). We can close off existing list items depending on the
amount of indentation:

  1) if there is no indentation, we close off /all/ list items;

  2) if there is maximum (already-seen) indentation (to line up with the
     most-nested list item), we don't close off any list items because we're
     just continuing the most-nested list item;

  3) otherwise, we have some amount of indentation and we only want to close off
     some list levels, depending on how much indentation we detect.

If there is more indentation than the maximum, then we are nesting another level
and don't need to close off anything. That's why this situation is not listed
above.

Point 1 is captured by =zeroIndent= in [[f:listItemsEndParser]]. Points 2 and 3 are
captured by =someIndent= in the same function.

#+name: f:zeroIndent
#+begin_src haskell
zeroIndent :: OrgParser (Int, Int)
zeroIndent = do
  orgParserState <- get
  _ <- MP.eof <|> MP.notFollowedBy (char ' ')
  let listLevelsToClose = length orgParserState.listLevels
      continuationIndent = 0 :: Int
  pure (listLevelsToClose, continuationIndent)
#+end_src

In [[f:zeroIndent]] we return the number of list levels to close off (in this case
all levels that we've detected so far), and the continuation indent. The
continuation indent is used by other parsers such as the [[f:paragraphParser]] to
see how to join up multiple lines into the same cell, assuming those lines have
the same continuation indent (the lines /continue/ if it's the same indent, hence
the name). For the case of [[f:zeroIndent]], we set the continuation indent to 0
because there is no indentation, and any subsequent lines belonging to the same
cell should /not/ have any indentation (it is considered invalid input if we see
surprise indentation; see [[t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over]]).

For =someIndent=, we just try to line up the amount of indentation found with the
existing list levels we have and how much indentation they expect.

#+name: f:someIndent
#+begin_src haskell
someIndent :: OrgParser (Int, Int)
someIndent = do
  orgParserState <- get
  indentFound <- T.length <$> MP.takeWhile1P (Just "indentation") (== ' ')
  let levelsToDropPerIndentationIncrement :: [(Int, Int)]
      levelsToDropPerIndentationIncrement =
        zip [0 ..]
          . map getContinuationIndentForLevel
          $ orgParserState.listLevels
      matchingIndent =
        find
          (\(_, continuationIndent) -> indentFound == continuationIndent)
          levelsToDropPerIndentationIncrement
  case matchingIndent of
    Nothing -> fail "indentation does not fit into existing levels"
    Just x -> pure x
#+end_src

It should be noted that the ~orgParserState.listLevels~ is grown by prepending to
the list in [[f:listItemNester]]. So the most-deeply-nested level will be at the
head of the list in ~orgParserState.listLevels~. That's why we use ~zip [0 ..]~ ---
we want to drop 0 levels if we see indentation matching the most deeply-nested
list item.

The [[f:getContinuationIndentForLevel]] just computes the total indentation for a
list level. For example, in

#+begin_src org
  1) foo
#+end_src

we compute the initial 2 spaces before the ~1)~, the ~1)~ itself (two characters),
plus a space character, or a total continuation indent of 5. The dynamic portion
here is the number, which will grow as the list grows (e.g., ~10)~ will have a
total continuation indent of 6), which is why we use a function here instead of
a constant.

#+name: f:getContinuationIndentForLevel
#+begin_src haskell
getContinuationIndentForLevel :: Level -> Int
getContinuationIndentForLevel level = level.indent + maybeNum + punctuation + space
  where
    punctuation :: Int
    punctuation = 1
    space :: Int
    space = 1
    maybeNum :: Int
    maybeNum = case level.number of
      Just n -> length $ (show n :: String)
      Nothing -> 0
#+end_src

Once we determine the amount of levels we need to close off (by parsing
[[f:zeroIndent]] or [[f:someIndent]]), we just go through the list markers we've already
seen in =listMarkers= and close them off via [[f:getEndingListMarkers]]. This is what
we do in [[f:listItemsEndParser]].

#+name: f:listItemsEndParser
#+begin_src haskell
listItemsEndParser :: OrgParser Cell
listItemsEndParser = do
  orgParserState <- get
  when (null orgParserState.listMarkers) $ do
    fail "no pending ListMarker objects to close off" -- (ref:list-items-end-failure-with-no-input)
  when (orgParserState.continuationIndent /= 0) $ do
    fail "not at beginning of line"
  (levelsToDrop, continuationIndent) <- zeroIndent <|> someIndent
  let markers = getEndingListMarkers levelsToDrop orgParserState.listLevels
  put
    orgParserState
      { listLevels = drop levelsToDrop orgParserState.listLevels,
        listMarkers = drop (levelsToDrop * 2) orgParserState.listMarkers,
        continuationIndent = continuationIndent
      }
  pure $ CListItem markers
#+end_src

In [[f:listItemsEndParser]], note that we initially fail the parser if there are no
pending =ListMarker= objects (no list levels to close off), or if we're not at the
beginning of the line (because otherwise we cannot measure indentation
accurately), by checking =orgParserState.continuationIndent=. This check of
=orgParserState.continuationIndent= is required because there is no easy way to
check for the just-parsed token (or character) in Megaparsec --- all parsers
only see what's in front of it. So then it's up to other parsers to set this to
some value greater than =1= (we do this in [[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]] which is
part of [[f:listItemStartParser]])

The =markersToDrop= is always twice the number of =levelsToDrop= because we always
add 2 for each level (see [[f:listItemNester]]).

If we detect the maximum allowed indentation, then there are no levels to drop,
and hence, we'll end up returning =CListItem []= which doesn't hold any meaning.
Avoiding this is a bit difficult; if we just fail the parser outright, then the
amount of parsed indentation will have to be parsed by another downstream parser
(this can get messy). So instead we just return =CListItem []= and then filter
these out in [[f:orgDocParser]]. This way we decide once and for all how to deal
with leading indentation (and how this closes off existing list items) in
[[f:listItemsEndParser]].

The [[f:getEndingListMarkers]] function understands how to close off list levels
based on whether the level uses an ordered or unordered list item.

#+name: f:getEndingListMarkers
#+begin_src haskell
getEndingListMarkers :: Int -> [Level] -> [ListMarker]
getEndingListMarkers levelsToDrop = concatMap f . take levelsToDrop
  where
    f :: Level -> [ListMarker]
    f level = case level.number of
      Nothing -> [LItemEnd, LUnorderedEnd]
      Just _ -> [LItemEnd, LOrderedEnd]
#+end_src

** Parsing list heads

In the [[id:5990dd7f-2f37-4a9e-96ea-6cc6b2e20dc0][previous section]] we considered how to close off list items based purely
on indentation. Parsing list heads is similar (because list heads can also close
off list items), but more complicated because we also have to take into account
how to create list items (list levels). This means we have to deal with all of
the possible values of [[data:ListMarker]] here.

Parsing the list head (and therefore the [[data:Level]] of a list item) is
straightforward. For unordered lists we just want to see a dash.

#+name: f:unorderedHeadParser
#+begin_src haskell
unorderedHeadParser :: OrgParser (Maybe Int)
unorderedHeadParser = do
  _ <- char '-'
  pure Nothing
#+end_src

For ordered lists we want to see the number and closing parenthesis.

#+name: f:orderedHeadParser
#+begin_src haskell
orderedHeadParser :: OrgParser (Maybe Int)
orderedHeadParser = do
  num <- MP.takeWhile1P (Just "ordered list number") isDigit
  _ <- char ')'
  let n = readMaybe (T.unpack num) :: Maybe Int
  when (isNothing n) $ do
    fail $ "could not parse " <> show (T.unpack num) <> " as Int"
  pure n
#+end_src

We check if the number looks like an =Int= with =readMaybe=.

For both cases we also parse leading indentation and also an additional space at
the end to separate the list head from the body. These considerations are
encoded in =listHeadIndentLevelParser= below.

#+name: f:listHeadIndentLevelParser
#+begin_src haskell
listHeadIndentLevelParser :: OrgParser Level
listHeadIndentLevelParser = do
  minimalIndentation
  spaces <- MP.takeWhile1P (Just "indentation") (== ' ')
  listHead <- unorderedHeadParser <|> orderedHeadParser
  let level =
        Level
          { indent = T.length spaces,
            number = listHead
          }
  afterListHead level
  pure level
  where
    minimalIndentation :: OrgParser ()
    minimalIndentation =
      void
        . MP.try
        . MP.lookAhead
        . MP.label "minimal indentation"
        $ MP.count 2 (char ' ')
    afterListHead :: Level -> OrgParser ()
    afterListHead level =
      void
        $ MP.choice
          [ MP.eof,
            void $ string "\n",
            void $ seeingContentAfterListHead level
          ]
    seeingContentAfterListHead :: Level -> OrgParser ()
    seeingContentAfterListHead level = do
      _ <- char ' '
      MP.notFollowedBy (char ' ')
      modify (\s -> s {continuationIndent = getContinuationIndentForLevel level})
#+end_src

The ~minimalIndentation~ expects there to be at least 2 spaces preceding a list
item. This follows Rule 1 from [[id:32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c][Detailed rules]].

The =afterListHead= parser preemptively checks what's in the input /after/ parsing
the list head. In the case where it sees some other non-empty content, it
expects some other parser to continue parsing. So inside
=seeingContentAfterListHead= we set the =continuationIndent= attribute to
indentation of the current level, because we expect another parser to help us
finish parsing the line. Then at the [[f:cellParser]] level, we set this back to =0=
for any parser that is expected to consume content and end with a newline. This
lets [[f:listItemsEndParser]] know whether it is looking at EOF or at the beginning
of a line when it inspects =continuationIndent=. If Megaparsec can let us easily
check the previous character that was parsed, this scheme of using a stateful
=continuationIndent= field wouldn't be necessary.

Note, however, that [[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]] only checks for textual
correctness. It doesn't check whether what's parsed is semantically correct. For
example, we wouldn't want to blindly parse the number of spaces in the indent
(instead we would want to check which indents are expected, given any previous
list items). Similarly we would want to check whether the list head number (for
ordered lists) is the expected (incremented) number from the previously-seen
list head number, if any (or if there is no previous number, that this number is
0 or 1).

We have to perform these additional checks by comparing the output of
=listHeadIndentLevelParser= against the existing [[data:OrgParserState]] (=listLevels=).
This is what we do in =listItemStartParser= below.

#+name: f:listItemStartParser
#+begin_src haskell
listItemStartParser :: OrgParser Cell
listItemStartParser = do
  _ <- MP.try $ MP.lookAhead listHeadIndentLevelParser
  orgParserState <- get
  let levels = orgParserState.listLevels
  level <- listHeadIndentLevelParser
  listMarkers <-
    if nestsBeyond levels level
      then listItemNester level levels
      else listItemFitter level levels
  pure $ CListItem listMarkers
  where
    nestsBeyond :: [Level] -> Level -> Bool
    nestsBeyond [] _ = True
    nestsBeyond (deepestLevel : _) level =
      getContinuationIndentForLevel deepestLevel == level.indent
#+end_src

The first line ~_ <- MP.try $ MP.lookAhead listHeadIndentLevelParser~ is needed
because otherwise as soon as Megaparsec sees any indentation (as part of
invoking [[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]]) it will expect to see a list head;
obviously this is not always the case as not all indentation leads to a list
head. This is why we have to perform this look-ahead preemptively.

The ~nestsBeyond~ function just checks whether the indentation we found in the
(just-parsed) level is the same as the continuation indent of the
most-deeply-nested level we have so far. This is how we nest one level deeper
than the current level. If this is the case, we delegate to [[f:listItemNester]].

Otherwise the list item is merely continuing some existing list, for which we
delegate to [[f:listItemFitter]].

** Nesting the list item beyond the current level

=listItemNester= appends starting markers to =listMarkers=. We use this information
in other places when we want to know how to un-nest an item (close it off),
such as in [[f:listItemsEndParser]] where we add corresponding closing markers with
=getEndingMarker=. The other place we use =listMarkers= is in [[f:listItemFitter]].

#+name: f:listItemNester
#+begin_src haskell
listItemNester :: Level -> [Level] -> OrgParser [ListMarker]
listItemNester level levels = do
  case level.number of
    Just 0 -> pure ()
    Just 1 -> pure ()
    Just n -> fail $ "starting list head number must be 0 or 1; got " <> show n
    Nothing -> pure ()
  orgParserState <- get
  let newMarker = case level.number of
        Just _ -> LOrderedStart
        Nothing -> LUnorderedStart
      opc = orgParserState.orgParserConf
  when (opc.maxListLevel > 0 && length levels == opc.maxListLevel) $ do
    fail
      $ "max nesting depth exceeded: cannot have more than "
      <> show opc.maxListLevel
      <> " levels of list items"
  put
    orgParserState
      { listLevels = level : levels,
        listMarkers = LItemStart : newMarker : orgParserState.listMarkers
      }
  pure [newMarker, LItemStart]
#+end_src

We enforce some additional rules here, such as forcing the nesting list head to
start off with either 0 or 1 (see [[t:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-0-or-1]]). The
rest of the function just updates the ~orgParserState~ to have the additional
~level~ as the new most-deeply-nested level, and also grows the ~listMarkers~
accordingly.

** Fitting the list item into an existing level

The goal of [[f:listItemFitter]] is twofold:

  1) continue an existing list, and

  2) close off (finish) pending levels which are still open.

The first task is easy --- we just append =[LItemEnd, LItemStart]= to signal that
we are closing off the existing list item (at the same level), and starting a
new list item (also at the same level).

The second task is a bit more involved. We have to "close" any existing levels
that are more deeply nested, just like in [[f:listItemsEndParser]]. For example, in
the following list

  1) foo

     - bar

       1) baz

  2) quux

when we hit "quux" we have to close off the list environments for "baz", then
"bar". However, unlike [[f:listItemsEndParser]], we have to /continue/ the list level
that we fit into. For ordered list items, this means keeping the latest
incremented ordered list item at the current level. This is why we have to drop
an extra level in ~listLevels~ in the ~fitter~ function, and then replace it with
the current level; if we don't do this then our ordered list items will never
get incremented.

#+name: f:listItemFitter
#+begin_src haskell
listItemFitter :: Level -> [Level] -> OrgParser [ListMarker]
listItemFitter level levels =
  case find (\(_, l) -> l.indent == level.indent) (zip [0 ..] levels) of
    Nothing -> fail "list item: invalid indentation"
    Just (levelsToDrop, lvl)
      | isJust level.number && isJust lvl.number ->
          let expectedNum = fmap (+ 1) lvl.number
           in if level.number == expectedNum
                then fitter levelsToDrop
                else
                  fail
                    $ "expected incremented list head "
                    <> (show . fromJust $ expectedNum)
                    <> " but got "
                    <> (show . fromJust $ level.number)
      | isNothing level.number && isNothing lvl.number -> fitter levelsToDrop
      | isJust lvl.number -> fail "expected ORDERED list item at this level"
      | otherwise -> fail "expected UNORDERED list item at this level"
  where
    fitter :: Int -> OrgParser [ListMarker]
    fitter levelsToDrop = do
      orgParserState <- get
      let markers = getEndingListMarkers levelsToDrop orgParserState.listLevels
      put
        orgParserState
          { listLevels = level : drop (levelsToDrop + 1) orgParserState.listLevels,
            listMarkers = drop (levelsToDrop * 2) orgParserState.listMarkers
          }
      pure $ markers <> [LItemEnd, LItemStart]
#+end_src

We use [[f:listItemStartParser]] and [[f:listItemsEndParser]] inside
[[f:cellParser]]; the former identifies and the beginnings of list items, while
the latter recognizes their (complete) end when there is no more indentation.

** Tests

There are [[id:32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c][so many rules]] associated with lists; we try to check a few of them
here.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-no-indent
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-no-indent
#+begin_src org
1)

2)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-no-indent
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-no-indent"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ plainParagraph "1)",
      plainParagraph "2)"
    ]
    []
#+end_src

The list-like text is parsed as plain paragraphs in
[[t:0009-list-indentation-no-indent]], because the lack of indentation means we
don't recognize the ~1)~ and ~2)~ as list items.

Here's a list with the (proper) 2-space indent.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-proper-indent
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-proper-indent
#+begin_src org
  1)

  2)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-proper-indent
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-proper-indent"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LItemStart
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Ordered lists require a closing parenthesis ~)~ after the number.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis
#+begin_src org
  1.

  2.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis"
  (utoks "." <> etoks ")" <> elabel "ordered list number")
  3
#+end_src

Ordered lists may only use numbers, not letters or Roman numerals.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters
#+begin_src org
  a)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters"
  "invalid indentation"
  2
#+end_src

Here's a nested example.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-nested
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-nested
#+begin_src org
  1)

     -

     -

  2)

     1)

        -

  3)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-nested
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-nested"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start outer ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start outer "1)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "2)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start of nested ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start of nested "1)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start deeply nested bullets.
          LItemStart -------- Deeply nested bullet start.
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Deeply nested bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of deeply nested bullets.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of nested "1)".
          LOrderedEnd, ------ End of nested ordered list.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "2)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "3)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "3)".
          LOrderedEnd ------- End outer ordered list.
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Nesting is limited to 5 levels, for a total of 6 levels by default.

#+name: ti:0009-list-nesting-max
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-nesting-max
#+begin_src org
  - 1

    - 2

      - 3

        - 4
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-nesting-max
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-list-nesting-max"
  "max nesting depth exceeded: cannot have more than 3 levels of list items"
  37
#+end_src

You can change this behavior with the ~lilac_maxListLevel~ [[id:84edd33a-a146-4a46-ac0a-cd1c3d0926d3][Lilac extension]]. In the
test below we increase the nesting level slightly.

#+name: ti:0009-list-nesting-max-8
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-nesting-max-8
#+begin_src org
,#+lilac_maxListLevel: 8
  - 1

    - 2

      - 3

        - 4

          - 5

            - 6

              - 7

                - 8

                  - 9
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-nesting-max-8
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-list-nesting-max-8"
  "max nesting depth exceeded: cannot have more than 8 levels of list items"
  156
#+end_src

We can prohibit list items entirely, much like how we can prohibit them in
[[t:0004-headings-prohibited]].

#+name: ti:0009-lists-prohibited
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-prohibited
#+begin_src org
,#+lilac_maxListLevel: -1
  - 1
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-prohibited
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-list-prohibited"
  "lists prohibited because lilac_maxListLevel < 0"
  29
#+end_src

Now we test whether list items can also hold content (other cells) inside it.

#+name: ti:0009-list-content-simple
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-content-simple
#+begin_src org
  1) First
     paragraph.

     Second paragraph.

  2) Another paragraph.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-content-simple
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-content-simple"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "First paragraph.",
      plainParagraph "Second paragraph.",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Another paragraph.",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Here's a more involved example using paragraph text for list items, following
the pattern from [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested]]. Note how the parsed list
structure is still there, except now we have =CParagraph= cells interspersed in
between them.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text
#+begin_src org
Foo.

  1) one

     - a

     - b

  2) two

     1) two-point-one

        hello

        - X

  3) three

Bar.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ plainParagraph "Foo.",
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start outer ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start outer "1)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "one",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      plainParagraph "a",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      plainParagraph "b",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "2)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "two",
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start of nested ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start of nested "1)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "two-point-one",
      plainParagraph "hello",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start deeply nested bullets.
          LItemStart -------- Deeply nested bullet start.
        ],
      plainParagraph "X",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Deeply nested bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of deeply nested bullets.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of nested "1)".
          LOrderedEnd, ------ End of nested ordered list.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "2)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "3)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "three",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "3)".
          LOrderedEnd ------- End outer ordered list.
        ],
      plainParagraph "Bar."
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Check whether we can detect invalid indentations for paragraphs that are
indented (indented due to them being inside a list). The first case is when the
line is indented too much in the list body.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
       bar
     baz.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body"
  (utoks " ")
  14
#+end_src

The same rule about keeping the same indentation applies for plain paragraphs
outside of list environments.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over
#+begin_src org
Foo
  bar
baz.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over"
  (utoks " ")
  4
#+end_src

Under-indentation is also a problem for continuation lines inside a single
paragraph block.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
   bar
     baz.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body"
  (utoks "b" <> etoks " ")
  12
#+end_src

If we have some number of list levels already, then the paragraph must fit into
one of those levels (it cannot have some arbitrary level of indentation) if
there is non-zero indentation.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
     bar.

 Invalid.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid"
  "indentation does not fit into existing levels"
  21
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-indent-levels
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-indent-levels
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
     bar.

     - Baz.

   Invalid.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-indent-levels
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-indent-levels"
  "indentation does not fit into existing levels"
  36
#+end_src

Paragraphs should be able to close list environments, depending on their
indentation. For example, in the test below the paragraph "Three" should be able
to close off the unordered list items.

#+name: ti:0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels
#+begin_src org
  1) One

     Two
     x

     - Bar

       - Baz

     Three
     a b
     c

Four
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "One",
      plainParagraph "Two x",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Bar",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Baz",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd,
          LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Three a b c",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Four"
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Paragraphs can close off list items, so we can mix ordered and unordered list
items at the same level, as long as the differing list type is closed off by a
paragraph. In the test below, the "Two" closes off the unordered list, so we're
allowed to start a new ordered list with =1) a=.

#+name: ti:0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists
#+begin_src org
  1) One

     - Bar

       - Baz

     Two

     1) a

     2) b

     Three

Four
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "One",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Bar",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Baz",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd,
          LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Two",
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "a",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "b",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Three",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Four"
    ]
    []
#+end_src

It's an error if there is no separation between list types (if we suddenly try
to change list types at the same level). In the test below, the =1) c= is
unexpected because the unordered list has not been closed off yet.

#+name: ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own
#+begin_src org
  - a

    - b

  1) c
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own"
  "expected UNORDERED list item at this level"
  21
#+end_src

The same applies for going the other way (trying to fit an unordered list into
an ordered one at the same level).

#+name: ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered
#+begin_src org
  1) a

     1) b

  - c
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered"
  "expected ORDERED list item at this level"
  23
#+end_src

List items have to be precisely indented; otherwise they'll hit an indentation
error. In the test below, the "1)" for the "b" item has only 4 spaces of
indentation instead of 5 (to line up with the "a" above it), so that should
trigger an indentation error.

#+name: ti:0009-lists-require-precise-indentation
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-require-precise-indentation
#+begin_src org
  1) a

    1) b
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-require-precise-indentation
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-lists-require-precise-indentation"
  "list item: invalid indentation"
  15
#+end_src

List items must be separated by at least one blank line. In the test case below,
there is not enough space so the "1) b" gets interpreted as regular paragraph
text, as part of the "a" above it.

#+name: ti:0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator
#+begin_src org
  1) a
     1) b
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "a 1) b",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Ordered list items at the same level must increment by one each time. In the
test case below, the "1) b" will fail to parse because it should be "2) b"
instead.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-lists-require-increment
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-lists-require-increment
#+begin_src org
  1) a

  1) b
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-lists-require-increment
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-ordered-lists-require-increment"
  "expected incremented list head 2 but got 1"
  13
#+end_src

Ordered list items must start from either 0 or 1.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-0-or-1
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-lists-start-from-0-or-1
#+begin_src org
  0) a

  1) b

     20) c
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-0-or-1
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-ordered-lists-start-from-0-or-1"
  "starting list head number must be 0 or 1; got 20"
  25
#+end_src

* Description lists, aka glossary terms
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       73acf466-6941-4c3b-9d6d-e0843d9b0480
:END:

[[https://orgmode.org/manual/Plain-Lists.html][Description lists]] are unordered list items that have a term followed by two
colons (~::~), and a prose description of that term. Structurally they are quite
similar to footnote definitions, which also have a leading ~name~ followed by the
~body~ (see [[data:Footnote]]).

The term "description list" comes from [[https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTML/Reference/Elements/dl][HTML]], and it would appear that Orgmode
borrowed the idea quite directly. For example, vanilla Orgmode will export the
following Org syntax

#+begin_src org
  - foo /hello/ :: bar

  - baz :: quux
#+end_src

to HTML as

#+begin_src html
<dl class="org-dl">
<dt>foo <i>hello</i></dt><dd>bar</dd>

<dt>baz</dt><dd>quux</dd>
</dl>
#+end_src

In HTML it is perfectly valid to have multiple ~<dt>~ (term) elements, or multiple
~<dd>~ (definition) elements, or both.

There are three differences worth noting for Lilac:

   1) Only one term and one description is allowed. If multiple terms mean the
      same thing, there should be multiple entries and all but one should
      redirect (with a link) to the entry that has the full definition. Multiple
      definitions for a single term are not allowed. Instead, you should use
      terms like ~foo (1) :: ...~ and ~foo (2) :: ...~ to define two different
      definitions for the same term.

   2) We don't call these description lists because that language is
      too close to the implementation detail. Instead we call them "glossary
      terms" (or simply "terms") because that's what they are used for most of
      the time.

      The other use case is for describing multiple key-value pairs,
      where all the pairs describe the same bigger concept. For example, the
      keys /Name/, /Birth date/, /City of birth/, etc could be used for describing a
      person. However Lilac does not support this use case.

   3) Lilac treats each key-value pair as a single [[data:Cell]]. And so, you can
      write them anywhere a cell is expected. So whereas Orgmode only allows
      them inside unordered lists, in Lilac you can write them as their own
      independent paragraphs, or in ordered lists, etc.

      This way Lilac can mix and match description list items with other items
      (it does not need the [[https://emacs.stackexchange.com/questions/17244/org-mode-list-with-a-mix-of-unordered-and-description-list-items][Orgmode workarounds described here]]). See
      [[id:eee95795-b51b-4de5-b39d-8dda436da451][how glossary terms are woven]] to see how this plays out during HTML export.

With those things out of the way, let's look at [[data:GlossaryTerm]], our take on
definition list entries:

#+name: data:GlossaryTerm
#+begin_src haskell
type GlossaryTerm :: Type
data GlossaryTerm = GlossaryTerm
  { term :: Prose,
    definition :: Prose
  }
  deriving stock (Eq, Ord, Generic, Show)
  deriving anyclass (FromJSON, ToJSON)
#+end_src

Glossary terms are created from [[newtype:Prose]] in [[f:convertToGlossaryTerm]]. A
paragraph is checked if it looks like a glossary term entry in
[[f:paragraphParser]], and delegates to [[f:convertToGlossaryTerm]] if so.

#+name: f:convertToGlossaryTerm
#+begin_src haskell
convertToGlossaryTerm :: Prose -> Int -> OrgParser Cell
convertToGlossaryTerm prose offset =
  toGlossaryTerm
    $ foldl' f ([], Prose [], []) prose.unProse
  where
    f (termPTokens, term'@(Prose finalTermPTokens), def) pToken
      | finalTermPTokens == [] =
          if isGlossaryTermDelimiter pToken
            then
              ( termPTokens,
                Prose . reverse $ dropWhitespacePToken termPTokens,
                def
              )
            else (pToken : termPTokens, term', def)
      | otherwise = (termPTokens, term', pToken : def)
    dropWhitespacePToken :: [PToken] -> [PToken] -- (ref:dropWhitespacePToken)
    dropWhitespacePToken [] = []
    dropWhitespacePToken tokens@(pToken : rest)
      | (PTokenStyledText styledText) <- pToken,
        Just (_, ' ') <- T.unsnoc styledText.body =
          rest
      | otherwise = tokens
    toGlossaryTerm :: ([PToken], Prose, [PToken]) -> OrgParser Cell
    toGlossaryTerm (_, term', def) = do
      let proseDefinition = compressProse . Prose $ reverse def
      assertUniqueGlossaryTerm (toText term') offset
      pure
        $ CGlossaryTerm
          GlossaryTerm
            { term = term',
              definition = proseDefinition
            }
#+end_src

[[(dropWhitespacePToken)]] removes any whitespace token just before the ~::~
delimiter. This is needed because otherwise the ~term~ string will end with
trailing whitespace.

[[f:convertToGlossaryTerm]] just checks each ~PToken~ in ~prose~ if it looks like a
glossary term delimiter, via [[f:isGlossaryTermDelimiter]].

#+name: f:isGlossaryTermDelimiter
#+begin_src haskell
isGlossaryTermDelimiter :: PToken -> Bool
isGlossaryTermDelimiter = \case
  PTokenStyledText styledText ->
    styledText.body == "::" && maskToStyles styledText.style == []
  _ -> False
#+end_src

The important bit here is the check of whether no styles are on when
encountering the string ~::~. This way, we don't interpret strings like

#+begin_src org
Foo ~a::b~ bar.
#+end_src

as a glossary term because the ~::~ will be inside the code (=~=) delimiters.

You may be thinking why we don't just use a raw string for the term (why do we
check each ~PToken~ individually in [[f:convertToGlossaryTerm]]?). The reasons is because
otherwise, a string like ~foo =a::b=~ will be interpreted as a glossary term of
~foo =a~ and a definition of ~b=~. So the search for the delimiter /must/ take into
account style markers, and the only way to do that is with ~PToken~ values.

#+name: f:assertUniqueGlossaryTerm
#+begin_src haskell
assertUniqueGlossaryTerm :: Text -> Int -> OrgParser ()
assertUniqueGlossaryTerm term offset = do
  orgParserState <- get
  when (member term orgParserState.glossary) $ do
    MP.setOffset offset -- (ref:glossary-term-offset)
    fail -- (ref:fail-because-duplicate-term)
      $ "glossary term "
      <> show term
      <> " is not unique"
  put
    orgParserState
      { OrgParserState.glossary =
          insert
            term
            orgParserState.cellNumber
            orgParserState.glossary
      }
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0015-glossary-duplicate-term
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0015-glossary-duplicate-term
#+begin_src org
foo :: 1

foo :: 2
#+end_src

#+name: t:0015-glossary-duplicate-term
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0015-glossary-duplicate-term"
  "glossary term \"foo\" is not unique"
  14
#+end_src

Originally we naively set the [[(glossary-term-offset)]] to be the beginning of the
paragraph (as seen by [[f:paragraphParser]]). However, this had the side effect of
making Megaparsec think that we had not consumed any input when failing at
[[(fail-because-duplicate-term)]]. So then Megaparsec would also report other parser
errors for those parsers that also did not consume any input and still signaled
a failure in [[f:cellParser]], namely in [[f:listItemsEndParser]]
([[(list-items-end-failure-with-no-input)]]). In order to suppress that error, we
changed [[f:textParser]] to set a ~glossaryTermOffset~ if it detected the glossary
term delimiter ([[(glossary-term-delimiter-detection)]]) [fn:megaparsec-errors].

** Printing glossaries
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e8ad30e5-5305-40e7-80ed-78bcec636ec1
:END:

Similar to [[id:e24de101-bf1d-418b-a18f-b1a98fea9696][Printing bibliographies]], we would like to allow users to print a
collection of all glossary terms found in the document (with links back to where
the term was originally defined --- for added context). We use the line
~#+print_glossary: local~ for this. The ~local~ just means printing the glossary
terms found in the current document. In the future we will allow this to be
~global~ to print all glossary terms found in the entire [[id:25827c17-2015-434b-b686-db3d72b2ee4d::data:Archive][data:Archive]].

#+name: f:glossaryLocationParser
#+begin_src haskell
glossaryLocationParser :: OrgParser Cell
glossaryLocationParser = do
  _ <- string "#+print_glossary: "
  isGlobal <-
    MP.choice
      [ string "local" >> pure False,
        string "global" >> pure True
      ]
  pure . CCommand $ PrintGlossary isGlobal
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0015-glossary-location
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0015-glossary-location
#+begin_src org
,#+print_glossary: local
#+end_src

#+name: t:0015-glossary-location
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0015-glossary-location"
  OrgDoc
    { oid = nilOid,
      cells = [CCommand $ PrintGlossary False],
      pseudoEdges = [],
      footnoteNumbers = M.empty,
      glossary = M.empty,
      lineLabels = M.empty,
      cslPath = Nothing,
      bibPaths = [],
      citations = [],
      meta =
        fromList
          [ ("ID", "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000")
          ]
    }
  defaultOrgParserState
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0015-glossary-location-global
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0015-glossary-location-global
#+begin_src org
,#+print_glossary: global
#+end_src

#+name: t:0015-glossary-location-global
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0015-glossary-location-global"
  OrgDoc
    { oid = nilOid,
      cells = [CCommand $ PrintGlossary True],
      pseudoEdges = [],
      footnoteNumbers = M.empty,
      glossary = M.empty,
      lineLabels = M.empty,
      cslPath = Nothing,
      bibPaths = [],
      citations = [],
      meta =
        fromList
          [ ("ID", "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000")
          ]
    }
  defaultOrgParserState
#+end_src

* Standalone math fragments
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d1064535-5c98-4c80-a901-5cdc1fd5297f
:END:

In vanilla Org, Latex fragments begin with =\begin{<ENVIRONMENT>}= and
=\end{<ENVIRONMENT>}=, where =<ENVIRONMENT>= can be anything. Presumably you could
inject images and illustrations; however those other non-mathematical facilities
are already supported by other Org features such as external links, so we don't
support them here.

Instead Lilac only supports Latex fragments for typesetting math. Because we
only support math, we call them /math/ fragments.

#+name: data:MathFragment
#+begin_src haskell
type MathFragment :: Type
data MathFragment = MathFragment
  { parentOid :: OID,
    meta :: Map Text Text,
    body :: Text
  }
  deriving stock (Eq, Generic, Ord, Show)
  deriving anyclass (FromJSON, ToJSON)
#+end_src

There are three ways to write math fragments: using =\begin{equation}= and
=\end{equation}=, =\[ ... \]=, or =\( ... \)=. The first two ways requires them to
start at the beginning of the line, while the third way allows them to be
embedded inside prose text (and displayed inside the paragraph). The third way
has already been discussed in [[f:mathFragmentInlineParser]], where it is used in
[[f:proseParser]]. So we limit our discussion here to parsing the first two ways,
which are /standalone/ math fragments. They are called standalone because they are
their own [[data:Cell][cells]] (as ~CMathFragment~ in [[f:mathFragmentStandaloneParser]]) and don't
need to be part of another cell.

Use the first form for a standalone equation like this

# Note: these code blocks for the math bits are needed for HTML audiences,
# because otherwise the markers like "\begin{equation}" and "\[" get removed by
# MathJax. That is, without these code blocks, HTML audiences will only see the
# rendered 2 + 2 = 4 output and not what goes into the inputs to generate them.

#+begin_src latex
\begin{equation}
2 + 2 = 4
\end{equation}
#+end_src

to get

\begin{equation}
2 + 2 = 4
\end{equation}

where the equation will get numbered and centered on its own. Use

#+begin_src latex
\[2 + 2 = 4\]
#+end_src

to get

\[2 + 2 = 4\]

to achieve the same effect, but without numbering. Finally, use inline math
delimiters like this: \(1 - 1 = 0\) which can be placed inside prose text (and
displayed inside the paragraph).

When using the standalone form, you can assign a =#+name= keyword to it to refer
to the equation using a human-friendly name, like this:

#+begin_src org
,#+name: eq:Pythagorean Theorem
\begin{equation}
a^2 + b^2 = c^2
\end{equation}
#+end_src

which becomes

#+name: eq:Pythagorean Theorem
\begin{equation}
a^2 + b^2 = c^2
\end{equation}

and you can link back to it using an internal link like [[eq:Pythagorean Theorem]].

Let's first see how to parse =\begin{equation}=.

#+name: f:mathFragmentNumberedParser
#+begin_src haskell
mathFragmentNumberedParser :: OrgParser MathFragment
mathFragmentNumberedParser = do
  orgParserState <- get
  let number = orgParserState.equationNumber + 1

  metaKVs <- metaParser
  _ <- lexeme $ string "\\begin{equation}"
  latex <-
    MP.someTill
      (MP.label "LaTeX standalone numbered math input" MP.anySingle)
      (string "\\end{equation}")

  modify (\s -> s {equationNumber = number})
  pure
    MathFragment
      { parentOid =
          firstParentOid orgParserState.docOid orgParserState.headingOidStack,
        meta =
          insert "__equation_number" (T.pack $ show number) metaKVs,
        body = trimTrailingWhitespace $ T.pack latex
      }
#+end_src

The call to [[f:trimTrailingWhitespace]] cleans up any trailing whitespace we parsed
into ~latex~.

#+name: f:trimTrailingWhitespace
#+begin_src haskell
trimTrailingWhitespace :: Text -> Text
trimTrailingWhitespace = T.dropWhileEnd (\c -> T.elem c " \n\r\t")
#+end_src

Parsing unnumbered equations is almost the same.

#+name: f:mathFragmentUnnumberedParser
#+begin_src haskell
mathFragmentUnnumberedParser :: OrgParser MathFragment
mathFragmentUnnumberedParser = do
  orgParserState <- get
  metaKVs <- metaParser
  _ <- lexeme $ string "\\["
  latex <-
    MP.someTill
      (MP.label "LaTeX standalone unnumbered math input" MP.anySingle)
      (string "\\]")
  anonNumber <- case lookup "name" metaKVs of
    Just _ -> pure Nothing
    Nothing -> do
      let n = orgParserState.anonEquationNumber + 1
      modify (\s -> s {anonEquationNumber = n})
      pure $ Just n
  pure
    MathFragment
      { parentOid =
          firstParentOid orgParserState.docOid orgParserState.headingOidStack,
        meta = case anonNumber of
          Just n ->
            insert "__equation_anon_number" (T.pack $ show n) metaKVs
          Nothing -> metaKVs,
        body = trimTrailingWhitespace $ T.pack latex
      }
#+end_src

We simply combine the numbered and unnumbered parsers together in the top-level
[[f:mathFragmentStandaloneParser]].

#+name: f:mathFragmentStandaloneParser
#+begin_src haskell
mathFragmentStandaloneParser :: OrgParser Cell
mathFragmentStandaloneParser =
  CMathFragment
    <$> (MP.try mathFragmentNumberedParser <|> mathFragmentUnnumberedParser)
#+end_src

Unlike [[f:tableLineParser]] and [[f:tableRowParser]], we don't use
~orgParserState.continuationIndent~ to check which lines are part of the math
fragment; we simply parse everything up until the end marker (~\end{equation}~ or
~\]~). This simplifies the parsing implementation. The downside is that we may
accept input where the indentation across multiple math lines looks messy, but
this is something the user should take care of anyway.

Let's check that we can parse multiple math fragments. We discard any trailing
whitespace after the initial opening =\begin{equation}= or =\[=, as well as trailing
whitespace for the contents inside (just before the =\end{equation}= marker).

#+name: ti:0010-standalone-math-fragments
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0010-standalone-math-fragments
#+begin_src org
\begin{equation}
1 + 1 = 2
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}



1 + 1 = 2



\end{equation}

\[
2 + 2 = 4
\]

,#+name: eq:x
\[


2 + 2 = 4
\]

,#+name: eq:y
\begin{equation}
1 + 1 = 2
\end{equation}
#+end_src

#+name: t:0010-standalone-math-fragments
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0010-standalone-math-fragments"
  ( nilOrgDoc
      [ CMathFragment
          MathFragment
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__equation_number", "1")
                  ],
              body = "1 + 1 = 2"
            },
        CMathFragment
          MathFragment
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__equation_number", "2")
                  ],
              body = "1 + 1 = 2"
            },
        CMathFragment
          MathFragment
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__equation_anon_number", "1")
                  ],
              body = "2 + 2 = 4"
            },
        CMathFragment
          MathFragment
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("name", "eq:x")
                  ],
              body = "2 + 2 = 4"
            },
        CMathFragment
